Daniel 	Tosh	expressed	his frustration	with	one	fraud	by	asking	his	audience,	“100,000	of	you	are	stupid enough	to	believe	this	hack	is	me?”	After	highlighting	several 	lame,	fake	tweets, Tosh	observed,	“It's	not 	a	parody	if	it’s	done	this	poorly!” Twitter	is	one	of	those	dangerous	toys	that 	if	it 	gets	in	the	hands	of	the	wrong person,	you'll 	have	the	mind	of	a	twelve-year-old	masquerading	as	an	adult. —Colin	Quinn Many	professionals	do	not 	use	Twitter	as	a	creative	medium.	However,	they recognize	that 	Twitter	is	a	great 	promotional 	and	marketing	tool.	Louis	C.K.	uses Twitter	to	publicize	his	concerts	and	promote	his	comedy	specials.	Although	he profits	from 	social 	media,	he	remains	ambivalent. I	kinda	hate	it.	I	think	it’s	awful.	I	have	Twitter	just 	so	I	can	tell 	people	what I want 	them 	to	buy,	and	give	me	money. —Louis	C.	K. Some	tech-savvy	comedians,	like	Jimmy	Kimmel 	and	his	talented	writing	staff, skillfully	use	Twitter	for	both	marketing	and	entertainment.	Kimmel 	created “#celebritymeantweets,”	a	comedy	bit 	that 	gives	celebrities	a	chance	to	read mean	things	people	tweet about 	them.	President 	Obama	read	the	following tweets	about 	himself	on	an	episode	of	 Jimmy	Kimmel 	Live! Is	there	anyway	we	could	fly	Obama	to	some	golf	course	halfway	around	the world	and	just 	leave	him 	there? —@RWSurferGirl A 	30	rack	of	color	light 	is	$23	now	at 	Sun	Stp.	Thanks,	Obama. —@Camoaignrpoz somebody	send	obama	some	life	hacks	on	how	to	be	a	good	president.	haha. like,	I	bet 	that 	would	help.	lol —@theautho Moreover,	like	most 	tweeters,	amateur	and	professional 	humor	writers	use Twitter	because	it’s	fun	and	a	great 	timewaster.	As	Evan	Williams,	Twitter founder,	said,	“Whoever	said	that 	things	have	to	be	useful?” Twitter	is	a	lot 	like	crystal 	meth,	because	it's	really	fun	to	do	and	Oprah's	on	it. —Bo	Burnham Handle:	 @Whoareyou Your	username,	or	handle,	is	your	digital 	identity	in	the	Twitterverse.	Ideally your	handle	should	be	unique,	eye-catching,	and	memorable.	The	following	are examples	of	funny	and	ingenious	handles: @MyAwkwardTweet @MyToaster @Masterofpuppets @Hamsterdeath @DepressedDarth @big_ben_clock @404UserNotFound With	a	billion	registered	Twitter	users,	there’s	a	good	chance	that your	favored name	is	not 	available.	That’s	why	most new	personal 	handles	are	names	or simple	variations	(@melhelitzer,	@mel_helitzer).	If	you	have	difficulty	choosing a	name,	there	are	username	generators	available	on	the	Web	that craft 	possible handles. The	 Writing	 Lab: @ComedyWritingSecretsistheGreatestBookEver Your	 username is	 your	 online first impression, and it establishes	 your digital	 identity. A good username is	 unique, real, and describes	 you. You might be a cat lover	 but @CatLover, @Lovescats, or @Kittenfriend are not especially	 distinctive. Moreover, avoid any inappropriate, gross, or	 sexist handle that might be blocked by others. Digital	 technology	 is	 both arousing and distancing. We don't look	 at the users	 on the other	 side as	 people. They	 aren't—they're just usernames, Facebook	 photos, and Twitter	 handles. —Douglas	 Rushkoff A username is	 often a POW. It can be as	 simple as	 reversing a name, like “kram”	 for	 “Mark”	 or	 “lem”	 instead of “Mel.”	 Or, it could be a literal	 description, such as	 Happy2BHere, EasyMommaNot, or WhatRULookinAt. If you have difficulty	 creating a name, the listing technique described in chapter	 nine can help you brainstorm	 ideas. Start by creating multiple categories, such as	 passions, hobbies, and personal	 characteristics. Then list as	 many	 items	 as	 you can within each category, and, when possible, create subcategories. Look	 at the lists	 and pair	 items. If you create a username that you love but is already	 used, consider	 adding a number	 or	 symbol	 to the name. If you are having username block, look	 at the unfortunate abuses of English from	 around the world at Engrish.com. English is	 a brutal language to learn (as	 evidenced by	 this	 book), and one misplaced word may	 transform	 an innocent message into an entirely	 different and funny	 meaning. We’re not mocking the writers	 but their malapropos	 may	 stimulate new	 username ideas. Size	 Matters:	 The	 Magic	 Number	 is	 140 Twitter’s	strictly	enforced	140-character	limit 	means	certain	humor,	like	the following	Craig	Ferguson	jokes,	would	not 	fit 	in	the	Twitterverse. Andy	Warhol 	said,	“In	the	future	everyone	will 	be	famous	for	fifteen	minutes.” Facebook	is	exactly	like	that except you’re	not 	really	famous	and	your	fifteen minutes	goes	on	forever. Some	people	use	Facebook	to	check	up	on	ex-boyfriends	or	ex-girlfriends.	That just 	seems	creepy	to	me.	I	like	it 	the	old-fashioned	way.	If	you	want 	to	check	up on	an	ex,	go	through	their	trash. In	 chapter	nine,	we	discuss	the	importance	of	word	economy	and	identified	the three	principles	of	aggressive	editing: 	Use	as	few	words	as	possible,	do	not reveal 	key	words	in	the	setup,	and	save	the	funniest 	word	for	the	end.	By aggressively	editing	and	using	abbreviations,	you	can	meet 	Twitter	standards while	maintaining	the	integrity	of	the	Ferguson	jokes. Andy	Warhol: 	“In	future	every1	will 	b	famous	for	15	mins.”	FB 	is	exactly	like that except ur	not 	really	famous	& 	ur	15	mins	goes	on	4ever. Some	ppl 	use	FB 	2	chk	up	on	ex’s.	That 	seems	creepy	2	me.	I	like	the	old fashioned	way.	If	u	want 	2	chk	up	on	an	ex,	go	through	their	trash. The	 Writing	 Lab:	 Word	 Economy Word economy	 and aggressive editing are critical	 humor	 writing skills	 for	 social	 media. Often fully	 formed jokes	 must be pared down before posting. Each of the following jokes	 about social	 media exceeds	 the 140.character	 Twitter	 limit (the number	 of characters	 is	 noted in the parentheses). Edit each joke so it could be posted on Twitter. A condensed version of each joke is	 at the end of the chapter. This	 week, all	 Facebook’s	 users	 will	 be asked to renew	 their	 privacy settings. In a related story, all	 Myspace users	 will	 be asked to get a life. (145) —Erik	 Bronstein A new	 study	 found that more than 11 million people have quit Facebook	 in the last three years. And unfortunately, none of them were your	 parents. (146) —Jimmy	 Fallon The United States	 has	 been using Twitter	 to help fight the Taliban. The theory	 is	 the Taliban will	 surrender	 if they	 read enough tweets from	 Kanye West. (152) —Conan O'Brien It's	 an interesting new	 feature. Soon you'll	 be able to find anything you want on Facebook, except for	 the thousands	 of hours	 of your	 life you lost going on Facebook. (166) —Jimmy	 Kimmel I think	 the love-hate is	 fundamental. Everyone hates	 reality television, and everyone's	 watching it. Everyone hates	 Facebook, and everyone is	 on it. (149) —Bo Burnham #funnysetups:	 Hashtags The	#	symbol 	is	used	to	create	a	Twitter	hashtag	that 	categorizes	tweets	and permits	for	the	searching	of	feeds	with	a	common	subject.	Extremely	popular hashtags	often	become	trending	topics	and	sometimes	go	viral.	For	example, Jimmy	Fallon	and	Justin	Timberlake	performed	a	 Late	Night 	with	Jimmy	Fallon sketch	parodying	hashtags	that went 	viral. #Twitter: 	proudly	promoting	ghastly	grammar	and	silly	misspelling	since	2006. —E.A.	Bucchianeri For	the	humor	writer,	a	hashtag	is	a	glorified	setup.	Fallon	created	“#latenight hashtags,”	an	interactive	comedy	bit 	that 	encourages	viewers	to	reply	to	his tweets.	Fallon	provides	the	joke	setup,	and	his	followers	respond	with	a	punch line.	Examples	of	hashtag	setups	include	“#WorseCarIEverHad,” “#FakeJayZLyrics,”	and	“#IGotCaught.”	The	following	are	sample	responses	to #MyWorstBirthday. They	forgot 	to	buy	candles	so	they	put 	Q-Tips	on	my	cake. —Carol Hart After	my	9th 	bday	everyone	who	had	the	cake	had	to	get a	shot 	bcuz	the	lady who	worked	at 	the	bakery	had	hepatitis. —Andrea	Galindo On	my	6th 	birthday	the	green	power	ranger	came	to	my	party.	He	got drunk	and told	us	that 	the	pink	ranger	was	a	huge	whore. —Rex	Ingram Facebook:	 Like	 Me, Please Like	Twitter,	Facebook	is	an	international 	social 	network	platform 	with	over	one billion	active	users.	While	Twitter	is	a	microblogging	tool 	for	sharing	ideas, Facebook	is	a	multipurpose	service	for	networking	people. Facebook	has	revealed	their	estimated	net 	worth—ninety-six	billion.	That's almost 	as	much	money	as	they	lose	every	year	from 	their	employees	wasting time	looking	at 	Facebook. —Jay	Leno Status:	 Headed	 to	 the	 Mall Most 	social 	networks	have	updates,	or	statuses,	which	allow	you	to	inform your friends	of	what 	you’re	doing.	Whether	it’s	to	announce	that 	you’re	breaking	up with	your	loser	boyfriend	or	to	explain	why	you	hate	Sam’s	Club,	the	idea	is	to stay	connected	24/7. Facebook's	new	relationship	status	option: 	"No	longer	able	to	interact 	with	actual people." —Andy	Borowitz Statuses	have	evolved	from 	daily	updates	to	a	forum 	for	one-liners.	The competition	for	posting	the	wittiest 	or	funniest 	status	has	spawned	websites devoted	to	highlighting	updates.	The	following	statuses	are	from Funnystatus.com. I	have	a	smartphone	with	a	dumbass	battery. A 	college	diploma	is	just 	a	receipt. There	is	a	reason	I’m 	single	and	it’s	called	my	face. The	 Writing	 Lab:	 Lmao The explosion of text-based communication spawned a telegraphic language. Sentences, phrases, and words	 are now	 truncated to speed communication. Many	 abbreviations, like "btw,”	 "fyi,"	 and "omg,"	 are universally	 recognized and incorporated into everyday lexicon. There are thousands	 of texting abbreviations, and they	 are tailored to specific	 audiences	 and platforms. For	 example, the shortcuts	 used by	 a gamer	 are different from	 the ones	 seen in an online dating chat room. The abbreviations	 desired by	 humor	 writers are lol	 (laugh out loud), lhh (laugh hella hard), rofl	 (rolling on the floor laughing), and lmao (laugh my	 ass	 off). The abbreviation most used by	 humorists	 is	 jk	 (just kidding). Whenever	 I'm	 on my	 computer, I don't type "lol."	 I type "lqtm": "laugh quietly	 to myself."	 It's	 more honest. —Demetri	 Martin Humor	 writers	 must be fluent in telegraphic	 writing, especially	 when using a forum	 like Twitter. Determine your	 texting IQ by	 decoding the following abbreviations. The answers	 are at the end of the chapter. 511 143 2EZ FF FWIW tweetsult NTS IANAL IMHO AYFKMWTS Blogging:	 Read Me, Please In	the	pre-Internet 	era,	publishing	an	article	or	book	followed	a	predictable course.	Authors	reviewed	 Writer’s	Digest 	for	potential 	outlets	and	then	submitted their	work	according	to	defined	guidelines.	The	process	varied	from 	publication to	publication,	but at 	some	point,	editors	analyzed	and	commented	on	the	article or	manuscript.	For	example,	three	different 	editors	reviewed	this	book,	and	their comments	resulted	in	7,896,352	changes	(might 	be	overstated,	slightly). The	main	advantage	of	the	traditional 	model 	is	that 	a	publication	is intensely	screened	before	it 	is	viewed	by	the	public.	That 	all 	changed	in	the	late 1990s,	with	the	development 	of	Web	publishing	software	that 	permitted	direct posting	of	material 	on	the	Internet.	These	“weblogs,”	now	called	“blogs,” allowed	authors	to	share	their	writing	with	others	and	bypass	outside	editing. Today	there	are	dozens	of	blogging	platforms	(WordPress,	Squarespace)	with hundreds	of	millions	of	blogs. Blogging	is	to	writing	what 	extreme	sports	are	to	athletics: 	more	free-form,	more accident-prone,	less	formal,	more	alive.	It 	is,	in	many	ways,	writing	out 	loud. —Andrew	Sullivan The	upside	of	blogging	is	that anyone	can	post 	material 	on	the	Web.	The downside	of	blogging	is	that anyone	can	post 	material 	on	the	Web.	Using	a variation	of	an	old	line,	the	question	becomes: If	you	write	humor	and	post 	on	it 	on	the	Internet,	and	no	one	sees	it,	is	it 	really humor	writing? The	humor	writing	principles	discussed	in	this	book	apply	to	blogs,	with	one exception: 	Internet 	pieces	must 	be	shorter	than	print 	articles.	The	physical 	layout of	a	Web	page,	and	the	short 	attention	span	of	the	typical 	Internet 	surfer,	both mandate	concision. Opening:	 Please	 Stay The	key	challenges	for	bloggers	are	attracting	and	keeping	Web	traffic.	The	most successful 	bloggers	recognize	that 	blogging	is	entertainment,	and	that 	the blogger	is	a	performer.	Like	all 	forms	of	entertainment,	humor	attracts	and maintains	attention. Since	bloggers,	like	stand-up	comics,	have	only	a	few	seconds	to	win	an audience,	headlines	must 	be	brief,	catchy,	and	funny.	If	readers	are	not hooked by	the	headlines,	they’ll 	quickly	move	to	the	next 	site.	Many	popular	and	well.written	humor	sites,	such	as	 McSweeney’s	Internet 	Tendency (www.mcsweeneys.net)	and	 The	Onion (www.onion.com),	specialize	in	witty,	off.the-wall 	titles,	like	these: Deadbeat 	Dads	March	on	Las	Vegas Goth	Kid	Builds	Scary-Ass	Birdhouse College	Student 	Does	Nothing	for	Tibet 	Over	Summer The	opening	lines	of	an	article	are	just 	as	critical 	as	headlines,	as	they	determine whether	the	reader	sticks	with	the	blog.	Unless	you’re	a	well-known	blogger with	an	established	base	of	readers,	you	should	front-load	your	best 	material. A great 	headline	mixed	with	a	lame	opening	is	like	inviting	someone	to	your house,	only	to	slam 	the	door	in	their	face	as	they	approach. —Brian	Clark MAPP the	 Blog Following	a	well-defined	MAPP 	is	critical 	to	the	sustained	and	continued success	of	a	blog.	If	humor	is	not 	targeted,	then	a	blog	may	become	a	glorified diary.	Moreover,	no	one	wants	to	read	a	diary. To	write	a	diary	every	day	is	like	returning	to	one's	own	vomit. —Enoch	Powell The	most successful 	bloggers	start 	by	picking	a	popular	topic—gossip,	politics, gadgets,	or	sex.	Then,	they	identify	their	audience. Reader	 Engagement:	 Putting	 out	 the	 Flames A 	stand-up	comic	gets	immediate	feedback	with	laughter	and	silence,	and	uses the	audience’s	responses	to	fine-tune	his	material.	The	criticism 	for	blogging	is neither	instantaneous	nor	always	representative.	However,	readers’ 	comments often	provide	bloggers	with	important feedback. Successful 	blogging	is	not about 	one	time	hits.	It’s	about 	building	a	loyal following	over	time. —David	Aston Readership	engagement 	determines	the	long-term 	success	of	a	blog,	and	reacting to	posts	is	critical 	for	maintaining	followers.	Responding	to	positive	feedback	is easy—affirm 	the	compliments	and	encourage	more	input—but 	dealing	with criticism 	is	challenging. While	stand-up	comics	deal 	with	hecklers,	bloggers	hassle	with	flamers. Flaming	happens	more	frequently	and	is	often	nastier	because	trolls	hide	behind the	anonymity	of	a	username.	Regardless	of	the	frequency	or	veracity	of	the posts,	there	are	two	ways	to	handle	mean-spirited	comments: 	Ignore	them or shoot 	them down. Have	people	always	been	this	angry?	I’ve	got 	a	funny	idea	that 	before	the Internet 	people	were	just 	writing	“fuck	you”	and	attaching	it 	to	pigeons. —Russell Howard Since	attention	is	fuel 	for	a	flamer,	if	they	don’t get 	a	reaction,	they	are	likely	to burn	out 	and	move	to	a	different 	blog.	Ignoring	a	flamer	necessitates	swallowing one’s	ego,	taking	the	hit,	and	being	the	“adult”	for	the	sake	of	the	blog. If	you	choose	to	respond	to	negative	comments,	it’s	critical 	to	remember that 	an	audience	is	watching.	Shooting	down	the	flamer	is	not 	the	main	goal; 	the objective	is	to	win	over	the	rest 	of	the	readership.	However,	if	you’re	not 	in	the mood	to	be	a	Good	Samaritan,	you	could	dope	slap	them 	like	stand-up	comics do	to	hecklers. Why	don't 	you	go	into	that 	corner	and	finish	evolving? —Russell Kane Did	your	mother	ever	tell you	not 	to	drink	on	an	empty	head? —Billy	Connolly Sorry,	I	can't 	understand	what 	you're	saying.	I'm 	wearing	a	moron	filter. —Arthur	Smith E-Mail:	 You’ve	 Got	 Mail It’s	hard	to	believe,	but 	electronic	mail 	predates	the	Internet.	Early	e-mail allowed	users	of	the	same	computer	to	send	messages	to	each	other.	After	the creation	of	the	Internet,	user-friendly	Web	interfaces	allowed	billions	of	e-mails to	be	sent 	daily. The	population	of	earth	has	reached	seven	billion	people,	every	single	one	of whom 	send	you	irritating	e-mails	to	join	something	called	“LinkedIn.” —Dave	Barry While	other	forms	of	electronic	messaging,	like	texting	and	social 	media,	have grown	in	popularity,	e-mail 	remains	the	most-used	feature	of	the	Internet.	For education	and	business,	e-mail 	is	the	primary	form 	of	electronic	communication. Signature Most 	e-mail 	has	a	sender’s	signature	that 	includes	name,	job	title,	contact information,	and	an	organizational 	logo.	Often	the	signature	contains	an inspirational 	or	motivational 	quote	by	someone	famous. Action	is	the	foundational 	key	to	all success. —Pablo	Picasso Failure	is	simply	the	opportunity	to	begin	again,	this	time	more	intelligently. —Henry	Ford It’s	better	to	give	than	to	receive.	Especially	advice. —Mark	Twain The	signature	could	include	uninspiring	quotes	from 	the	infamous. Accept 	that 	some	days	you	are	the	pigeon	and	some	days	the	statue. —Dilbert For	just 	one	night 	let’s	not 	be	co-workers.	Let's	be	co-people. —Ron	Burgundy If	something’s	hard	to	do,	then	it’s	not 	worth	doing. —Homer	Simpson The	signature	provides	a	golden	opportunity	to	use	POW 	to	create	fictitious quotes. The	problem 	with	quotes	on	the	Internet 	is	you	can	never	be	certain	they're authentic. —Abraham 	Lincoln Your	signature	should	be	tailored	to	your	e-mail 	audience.	It’s	doubtful your boss	would	appreciate	a	signature	sign-off	from 	South	Park’s	Eric	Cartman	like “Don’t 	have	a	cow,	man!”	or	“Respect 	my	authority.” Grammar	 Police CollegeHumor 	featured	an	article,	“No	One	Really	Knows	How	to	Write	an Email.”	Here’s	the	article	in	its	entirety: 	“Bcc: everyone.” Most 	forms	of	electronic	messaging,	including	Twitter	and	texting, encourage	bad	grammar	and	misspellings.	Since	e-mail 	is	the	most 	formal form of	electronic	communication,	there’s	no	need	to	abuse	the	English	language. Moreover,	there	is	no	reason	to	disrespect 	the	funny	with	shortcuts—all 	the techniques	and	principles	described	in	 chapter	eleven,	Print Humor,	work	in	e.mail. Humor	 Sells With	consumers	bombarded	by	promotional 	e-mail,	humor	can	humanize	a company’s	brand.	Retailers,	such	as	Zappos	and	Moosejaw,	regularly	use	humor in	their	e-mail 	marketing	campaigns	with	an	abundance	of	nonsequiturs	and	one.liners: How’s	my	hair	doing? Check	us	out.	Or	don’t. Deals	so	decent 	you	will want 	to	lick	the	screen. Online	 Dating:	 Pick	 Me, Please Al 	Gore	“created”	the	Internet 	to	network	Defense	Department 	computers.	It’s highly	doubtful 	that 	Gore	or	anyone	else	working	on	packet 	switching	thought they	were	inventing	the	ultimate	dating	service. Computer	dating	is	fine,	if	you're	a	computer. —Rita	Mae	Brown Online	dating	is	a	billion-dollar	business	with	over	fifty	million	consumers. Dating	services	range	from 	the	megasized	(Match,	eHarmony,	Gay)	to	targeted (JDate,	OurTime,	BBW 	Cupid)	sites.	Now	there	are	real-time	hookups	available via	Grindr	and	Tinder.	About 40	percent 	of	all 	singles	have	used	online	dating services,	and	the	users	with	the	greatest 	number	of	dates	bought at 	least 	five copies	of	 Comedy	Writing	Secrets. It 	said	if	you're	not 	involved	in	one	of	these	online	dating	services,	you're	just gonna	get 	left 	behind	socially,	you	know.	I'm not 	afraid	to	try	something	new, I'm 	not,	so	I	signed	up.	Changed	my	life.	I	went from 	feeling	pretty	good	about myself	to	feeling	like	a	leper	alone	in	a	room 	typing. —Ryan	Hamilton In	the	marketplace	of	love,	the	product 	is	you.	We’re	not 	being	crass	or minimizing	the	challenges	of	meeting	others,	but 	online	dating	is	a	form 	of	sales. And,	like	any	business,	advertising	is	the	key	to	success.	Whether	it’s	a	six-pack of	Diet 	Mountain	Dew	or	you,	humor	promotes	sales. Humor	is	not 	only	critical 	to	your	personal 	marketing	campaign,	it’s	also	a highly	desired	commodity	in	the	dating	arena.	Survey	after	survey	of	online	(and real-time)	daters	found	that 	both	men	and	women	listed	“sense	of	humor”	as	a “must-have.”	Women	value	a	sense	of	humor	because	it 	predicts	happiness	and life	balance.	Men	want 	someone	to	laugh	at 	their	jokes.	Humor	is	an	invaluable tool 	for	creating	a	personal 	ad	that 	will 	interest 	others	and	increase	the	chances of	being	contacted. The	 Headline A personal 	ad	headline	is	like	a	storefront—it 	should	grab	prospective	buyers’ attention	and	make	them 	curious. The	best 	headlines	are	cute	and	catchy. Recommended	by	three	out 	of	four	doctors. Looking	for	my	prize	trout,	not 	the	usual carp. 30-day	free	trial 	period. Lame	headlines	are	sexist,	transparent,	and	sleazy. Stallion	on	Viagra. Looking	for	shining	knight 	with	big	sword. Slicker	than	KY 	jelly. Corporations	use	slogans	to	advertise	and	brand	their	products.	Sample catchphrases	include	“Works	like	a	dream”	(Ambien),	“Be	all 	that you	can	be” (U.S.	Army),	and	“Get 	the	feeling”	(Toyota).	Look	at 	corporate	advertising slogans	and	identify	your	favorites.	Then	reform 	the	lines	to	fit 	your	brand.	That is,	you. The	 Profile The	tone,	style,	and	humor	of	the	ad	should	match	the	headline.	If	the	headline	is whimsical,	then	the	profile	should	be	whimsical.	Remember,	you’re	selling	a brand,	and	your	writing	should	mirror	what 	you’re	selling. My	greatest 	strength	is	common	sense.	I'm 	really	a	standard	brand—like Campbell's	tomato	soup	or	Baker's	chocolate. —Katherine	Hepburn If	you	use	humor	in	the	profile,	there’s	only	one	suitable	humor	target—yourself. Other	topics	are	too	high-risk	because	the	audience	is	unknown.	And	self.deprecating	humor	should	not 	be	overdone—you	don’t want 	to	diminish	the brand. While	humor	can	make	your	profile	engaging	and	unique,	funny	alone	will not 	sell 	your	brand.	In	fact,	too	much	humor	can	make	it 	unmarketable. If	you're	single,	you	might want 	to	listen	to	this.	I	read	about 	a	new	dating website	that 	lets	you	get 	to	know	people	first 	before	you	see	their	picture. Because	if	there's	anything	better	than	love	at 	first 	sight,	it's	falling	OUT 	of	love at 	first 	sight. —Jimmy	Fallon Prospective	mates	want 	someone	with	a	sense	of	humor	but are	not 	looking	for	a stand-up	comic.	A personal 	ad	with	too	many	jokes	or	sarcastic	statements	will come	across	as	forced,	desperate,	or	overkill. Throughout 	the	book,	we’ve	discussed	the	importance	of	honesty—the most 	effective	humor	is	real,	genuine,	and	truth	based.	We’ve	also	explained	that humorists	take	the	truth	and	stretch	it 	for	comedic	purposes.	When	it 	comes	to online	dating	profiles,	embellishment 	is	not 	a	good	thing.	If	you	exaggerate, you’ll 	be	discovered	and	others	will 	be	disappointed.	In	the	marketplace	of	love, honesty	trumps	funny. The	 Writing	 Lab:	 Answers The following are the condensed jokes. This	 wk, all	 FB’s	 users	 will	 b asked 2 renew	 their	 privacy	 settings. In a related story, all	 Myspace users	 will	 b asked 2 get a life. (134) —Erik	 Bronstein A new	 study	 found that more than 11M	 ppl	 have quit FB in the last 3 yrs. Unfortunately, none of them	 were ur	 parents. (118) —Jimmy	 Fallon The US has	 been using Twitter	 2 help fight the Taliban. The theory	 is the Taliban will	 surrender	 if they	 read enuff tweets	 from	 Kanye West. (139) —Conan O'Brien Its	 an interesting new	 feature. Soon ull	 b able 2 find anything u want on FB, except 4 the 1000s	 of hours	 of ur	 life u lost going on FB. (138) —Jimmy	 Kimmel I think	 the love-hate is	 fundamental. Evry1 hates	 reality	 TV, & evry1's watching it. Evry1 hates	 FB, & evry1 is	 on it. (119) —Bo Burnham These are the deciphered text abbreviations. 511 =	 too much information (more than 411) 143 =	 I love you 2EZ =	 too easy FF =	 follow	 Friday FWIW	 =	 for	 what it’s	 worth tweetsult =	 insult sent using Twitter NTS =	 note to self IANAL =	 I am	 not a lawyer IMHO =	 in my	 honest opinion AYFKMWTS =	 Are you fucking kidding me with this	 shit? Recap The	Internet 	forever	changed	humor	writing.	For	the	most 	part,	the	changes	are positive	for	writers.	Now	anyone	can	post 	anything,	pretty	much	anywhere, which	might 	be	read	by	everyone.	That 	includes	the	good,	the	bad,	and	the	wtf. Blame	Al 	Gore.	jk. The	day	I	made	that 	statement,	I	was	tired	because	I	had	been	up	all 	night inventing	the	camcorder. —Al Gore Chapter	 13 Teach,	Learn,	and	Laugh A 	very	wise	old	teacher	once	said,	“I	consider	a	day's	teaching	wasted	if	we	do not 	all 	have	one	hearty	laugh.” —Gilbert 	Highet Humor	is	an	educational 	lubricant.	Whether	it’s	a	K-12,	college,	or	online classroom,	humor	makes	learning	more	enjoyable,	engaging,	and	memorable. Moreover,	humor	allows	teachers	to	model 	and	reinforce	a	critical 	educational lesson—learning	is	fun. Pedagogical 	humor	is	not about 	field	trips,	movie	days,	or	dodge	ball. Humor	as	an	instructional 	tool 	creates	a	positive	classroom 	environment 	that opens	minds,	fosters	communication,	and	encourages	active	participation.	In addition,	humor	can	provide	the	teacher	and	students	an	important 	necessity	of teaching	and	learning—a	“mental break.” It’s	not 	what’s	taught,	but 	what’s	caught.	And	if	we	can	get 	our	students’ 	mouths open	for	laughter,	we	can	slip	in	a	little	food	for	thought. —Virginia	Tooper Humor	is	a	proven	strategy	for	improving	instruction	and	increasing	retention. Studies	show	that 	humor	enriches	learning	by	increasing	student 	interest and attention—making	even	the	driest 	subjects	come	to	life.	Humor	also	reduces anxiety	concerning	challenging	subjects	and	makes	difficult 	concepts	clearer	and more	memorable.	In	addition,	humor	ultimately	promotes	creativity,	exploration, and	critical 	thinking—skills	that 	students	can	carry	with	them 	for	a	lifetime. The	growing	interest 	in	educational 	humor	affords	new	opportunities	for humor	writers.	Publishers	of	educational 	and	business	training	materials,	such	as books,	seminar	handouts,	and	instructional 	programs	are	increasingly	seeking writers	whose	light 	touch	can	change	dreaded	subjects	into	interesting	and memorable	ones.	John	Cleese	of	Monty	Python	fame	used	humor	to	teach management 	principles	in	his	highly	successful 	line	of	business	training	films. His	instructional 	philosophy	was	simple. He	who	laughs	most,	learns	best. —John	Cleese A Classroom	 Is	 Not	 A Comedy	 Club When	used	appropriately,	as	noted	above,	humor	can	reduce	student 	stress, enhance	comprehension,	and	promote	critical 	thinking.	However,	humor	can	also be	an	educational 	irritant.	Humor	that 	is	derogatory	or	ridiculing	has	no	place	in an	educational 	setting.	Also,	too	much	humor	is	distracting	and	unneeded.	The judicious,	developmentally	appropriate,	and	timely	use	of	humor	makes	learning memorable	and	enjoyable. I	think	you	learn	more	if	you're	laughing	at 	the	same	time. —Mary	Ann	Shaffer There	are	three	major	considerations	when	writing	instructional 	humor. Does	the	humor	promote	an	educational 	objective? Will 	the	students	understand	and	appreciate	the	humor? Is	the	humor	target 	appropriate	for	the	classroom? Learning	 Ain’t	 Fluff When	writing	instructional 	humor,	the	most 	important 	consideration	is	the educational 	purpose	of	the	humor.	While	humorists	gauge	success	by	laughter, educators	measure	the	effectiveness	of	humor	by	how	it 	promotes	learning. Although	humor	can	enhance	students’ 	educational 	experience,	most 	of	the humor	written	for	a	course	should	serve	an	instructional 	purpose.	Off-topic humor	might 	be	funny,	yet 	the	students	may	view	the	instructor	and	course material 	as	fluff. A 	professor	is	one	who	talks	in	someone	else’s	sleep. —W.H.	Auden There	are	two	important 	reasons	why	humor	writers	and	instructors	should	not seek	or	expect 	big	laughs.	First,	powerful 	humor	can	often	overshadow	a learning	objective.	If	a	concept 	is	illustrated	with	a	very	funny	story,	students	are likely	to	remember	the	humor	and	forget 	the	principle.	Second,	the	structure	of the	typical 	joke	(a	setup	followed	by	a	punch	line)	limits	the	funniness	of pedagogical 	humor.	If	instructors	use	topically	relevant 	humor,	then	they	lose	the flexibility	of	exaggerating	or	distorting	the	setup	to	maximize	the	funny. The	educational 	context 	also	changes	humor	expectations.	Audiences expect 	comedians	to	be	funny; 	students	expect 	teachers	to	be	scholarly,	serious, and	often	boring.	The	diminished	expectations	lower	the	comedic	threshold— simpler	forms	of	humor,	like	POW 	or	witty	observations,	which	would	“bomb” in	a	comedy	venue,	will 	work	in	a	classroom.	Since	students	anticipate	boredom, they	will 	appreciate	any	attempt at 	humor	and	view	the	teacher	as	striving	to make	the	course	more	interesting. I	always	loved	art 	teachers	because	they	were	so	bizarre.	They	were	like	the homeless	people	of	the	faculty—all 	disheveled,	wearing	smocks,	covered	in paint,	always	digging	through	the	garbage,	looking	for	bottles	and	egg	cartons and	things. —Ted	Alexandro Instead	of	aiming	for	the	biggest 	possible	laugh,	writers	need	to	tamper	down their	jokes.	Strong	humor	may	pacify	confused	students,	leaving	them 	less	likely to	seek	clarification	when	necessary.	Also,	students	might 	assume	that 	getting	a joke	means	they	understanding	the	concept.	Learning	should	be	fun	but 	isn’t necessarily	easy. MAPP the	 Students The	audience	ingredient 	of	the	MAPP 	theory	is	critical 	in	educational 	humor. The	material 	must 	be	student-oriented	and	factor	in	cultural,	gender,	and	age differences.	A 	joke	that 	works	in	one	setting	for	one	student 	group	may	fail 	in another.	In	addition,	the	humor	must 	also	fit 	the	subject 	and	reflect 	the	teacher’s personality. There	are	three	things	to	remember	when	teaching: 	know	your	stuff; know whom 	you	are	stuffing; 	and	then	stuff	them 	elegantly. —Lola	May It’s	also	critical 	to	recognize	developmental 	differences	in	humor	appreciation. Children	in	kindergarten	through	sixth	grade	delight 	in	wordplay	such	as	puns, comic	verse,	and	riddles.	The	cornier	the	joke,	the	better	the	response. Knock	Knock Who’s	there? Ada! Ada	who? Ada	burger	for	lunch! The	same	humor	for	older	children	might 	produce	the	“teacher	is	an	idiot”	stare. Satire,	parody,	and	irony	are	more	suitable	for	high	school 	students	and	adult learners.	Older	students	will 	appreciate	the	irony-based	“Murphy’s	Laws	of Applied	Terror.” When	reviewing	your	notes	before	an	exam,	you’ll 	discover	that 	the	most important 	ones	are	illegible. If	you’re	given	an	open-book	exam,	you’ll 	forget your	book. If	you’re	given	a	take-home	exam,	you’ll 	forget 	where	you	live. When	possible,	the	demographics	and	interests	of	the	students	should	be identified	before	writing	humor.	This	can	be	challenging,	given	the	diversity	of the	typical 	classroom,	and	is	almost 	impossible	for	distance	learning,	such	as correspondence	or	Internet courses. The	 PC	 Hall Monitors Educational 	humor	must 	be	inclusive	and	avoid	any	appearance	of	ridiculing others	or	stereotyping	groups.	Sexist,	racist,	derogatory,	or	obscene	humor	is never	justified.	Even	a	flippant 	remark	might 	be	viewed	as	offensive. Since	the	PC 	police	wander	the	instructional 	hallways	 24⁄7 	looking	for potential 	transgressions,	a	simple	literal 	truth	joke	based	on	the	word	 slow 	will surely	be	deemed	offensive. I	live	near	a	remedial 	school.	There	is	a	sign	that 	says,	“Slow	...	children.”	That can't 	be	good	for	their	self-esteem.	But,	look	on	the	positive	side,	they	can't read it. —Jimmy	Carr Even	a	joke	about 	political 	correctness	may	be	red	flagged	because	it 	makes light 	of	academic	failure. As	a	teacher	it’s	important 	to	be	politically	correct.	I’ve	learned	not 	to	tell a student 	they’re	failing	my	class,	but 	rather	that 	they	will 	have	another	semester in	which	to	get 	to	know	me	better. —Bonnie	Cheeseman Given	the	double-edged	nature	of	humor	and	the	vigilance	of	the	PC 	police,	the safest 	target 	is	always	the	instructor.	Self-deprecating	humor	puts	students	at ease	and	avoids	belittling	or	alienating	others.	The	“Oops,	that 	was	a	stupid remark!”	response	to	a	mistake	by	the	teacher	allows	students	to	view	the instructor	as	more	human. Some	of	you	might 	have	heard	that I	am 	unfair,	rigid,	and	boring.	For	those	of you	who	have	not 	talked	to	my	wife	… I	am 	so	clever	that 	sometimes	I	don't 	understand	a	single	word	of	what I	am saying. —Oscar	Wilde I	know	as	a	teacher	I	should	use	self-deprecating	humor,	but 	I	really	suck	at telling	jokes	about 	myself. Another	safe	target 	is	academic	subject 	matter.	It’s	highly	doubtful 	that 	the following	math	jokes	will 	trigger	PC 	alarms. Two	random 	variables	were	talking	in	a	bar.	They	thought 	they	were	being discrete,	but 	I	heard	their	chatter	continuously. Arithmetic	is	the	art 	of	counting	up	to	twenty	without 	taking	off	your	shoes. There	are	three	types	of	mathematicians: 	Those	who	can	count 	and	those	who can't. Teachers	 Do It	 at	 the	 Podium:	 Instructional Humor Effective	lectures,	presentations,	and	workshops	follow	the	same	principles	as speeches.	Whether	you’re	speaking	to	third	graders	about 	social 	studies,	to	tenth graders	about 	economics,	or	to	corporate	employees	during	a	manager’s	training session,	you	have	to	stay	on	message.	Use	humor	to	supplement 	your	message— not 	overshadow	it.	Here	are	a	few	techniques	you	can	use	when	you’re	up	at 	the podium. Open	 With	 a	 Bang Students	will 	quickly	stereotype	teachers.	Opening	with	a	joke	or	funny	quote sends	the	message,	“This	will 	be	fun,	so	pay	attention.”	As	Herbert Gardner noted,	“Once	you	get 	people	laughing,	they’re	listening.” I	will 	never	forget 	my	first 	day	of	school.	My	mom 	woke	me	up,	got 	me	dressed, made	my	bed,	and	fed	me.	Man,	did	the	guys	in	the	dorm 	tease	me. —Michael 	Aronin Smartness	runs	in	my	family.	When	I	went 	to	school,	I	was	so	smart 	my	teacher was	in	my	class	for	five	years. —Gracie	Allen I	had	a	terrible	education.	I	attended	a	school 	for	emotionally	disturbed	teachers. —Woody	Allen Other	possible	openers	include	a	silly	subtitle,	a	visual 	or	cartoon	with	a humorous	caption,	inserting	funny	fictitious	names	in	the	roll 	call,	exaggerated unit 	objectives,	a	reformatted	quote,	or	a	witty	remark	introducing	the	subject. Today’s	lecture	will 	be	an	experiment—half	of	you	will get real 	information, while	the	other	half	will get 	a	placebo. —Matt 	Coleman When	I	give	a	lecture,	I	accept 	that 	people	look	at 	their	watches,	but what I	do not 	tolerate	is	when	they	look	at 	it 	and	raise	it 	to	their	ear	to	find	out 	if	it stopped. —Marcel Achard Our	topic	today	is	déjà	vu: 	Stop	me	if	you’ve	heard	this	before. A 	fun	icebreaker	that works	at 	any	level 	and	ensures	group	interaction	is	the Find	Someone	Who 	activity.	Prepare	a	handout 	that 	requires	students	to	obtain classmates’ 	names	based	on	physical 	features	(someone	who	is	taller,	someone who	has	the	same	color	hair)	and	personal 	preferences	(someone	who	likes	the same	music	or	lives	in	the	same	neighborhood).	Also	include	comical 	items (someone	who	took	a	shower	this	week)	and	items	that 	pertain	to	the	class subject. Bridge	 the	 Gap When	eyes	begin	to	glaze	over,	it’s	time	to	shift 	gears.	As	a	transitional 	device, humor	gives	students	a	mental 	break.	The	trick	is	to	sandwich	the	humor: 	Teach a	concept,	use	humor	to	regain	student 	attention,	and	summarize	the	principle. To	be	effective,	humor	transitions	need	to	be	short.	If	you	use	an	anecdote, remember	to	keep	it 	brief.	If	the	transition	takes	too	long,	it 	can	be	difficult 	to calm 	students	down	and	return	to	the	topic. I	used	to	substitute	teach.	The	worst.	I	will 	never	forget 	this	second-grade	class where	I	was	subbing	for	a	teacher	named	Susan. All 	day	long	these	annoying	little	children	would	say,	“Susan	doesn’t 	do	it 	that way.	Susan	lets	us	play.	Susan	gives	us	gum.	Susan	is	prettier	than	you.” “Oh,	really?	Susan’s	dead.” —Cathy	Ladman The	 Unexpected	 One-Liner Teachers	can	prepare	one-liners	for	when	a	class	is	interrupted	by	an	unexpected event.	(These	are	similar	to	savers	prepared	for	speeches	and	live	performances.) A 	plane	flies	overhead: 	Here’s	my	ride. AV 	equipment	fails: 	That’s	what happens	when	you	buy	from 	eBay. Cell 	phone	goes	off: 	For	me? Leave	 ’Em	 Laughing Longer	humor	pieces,	such	as	humorous	exercises,	are	best 	suited	for	wrapping up	a	subject 	lesson	or	ending	the	day.	A 	popular	closer	is	a	top	ten	list. Top 	Ten 	Excuses	for 	Submitting	a	Late	Homework 	Assignment 10.	I	thought 	Groundhog	Day	was	a	national 	holiday. 9.	My	pit 	bull 	ate	my	ferret,	which	ate	my	assignment. 8.	My	mom 	didn’t 	have	the	time	to	finish	my	homework. 7.	I	gave	my	assignment 	to	Bart 	to	copy,	but 	he	never	returned	it. 6.	I	thought I	was	ADD; 	now	I	know	I	am. 5.	My	assignment 	was	confiscated	by	TSA 	workers	for	being	too	long. 4.	I’m 	living	in	my	parents’ 	basement 	until 	they	die,	so	what’s	the	point? 3.	It 	took	a	long	time	to	find	the	answers	on	the	Internet. 2.	I	accidentally	drank	a	six-pack	of	beer	and	thought 	I	had	already graduated. 1.	I	told	Siri 	to	do	the	assignment,	but 	she	forgot. Hand	 Out	 the	 Funny I	like	a	teacher	who	gives	you	something	to	take	home	to	think	about 	besides homework. —Lily	Tomlin Humor	can	add	spice	to	syllabi,	handouts,	and	other	materials	distributed	during presentations.	Examples	of	POWs	used	in	handouts	include	silly	names,	amusing titles	or	headings,	oxymorons,	and	fictitious	terms	or	definitions.	Exaggerated humor	examples	include	self-effacing	humor; 	distorted	numbers,	concepts,	or phrases; 	outrageous	theories	or	studies; 	and	whimsical 	rules	and	procedures. There	is	no	penalty	for	leaving	class	early,	but 	you	will 	be	mocked	and	nasty comments	will 	appear	on	your	Facebook	page. The	following	are	prohibited	during	lecture: 	phone	calls,	texting,	and	twerking. There	is	no	such	thing	as	a	dumb	question	unless	the	question	begins	with	“This might 	be	a	dumb	question	but.	…” The	 Writing	 Lab:	 On	 the	 Couch The steps	 for	 writing educational	 humor	 are the same as	 for	 writing any	 other	 joke. Let’s	 write a joke with the setup Sigmund Freud’s	 Pet Peeves. Start by	 listing things	 associated with Freud: the couch, patients free-associating, or	 looking at inkblots. Then identify	 the opposite of each item: clients	 sleeping instead of talking, clients	 drawing on the inkblots. By	 combining the associations	 and disassociations, you’d able to create the following triple. Sigmund Freud’s	 major	 pet peeves	 were patients	 who fell	 asleep on the couch, drew	 on the inkblots, and complained about being charged for	 free association. The joke is	 no gut buster, but it does	 meet the criteria for	 effective educational	 humor—it relates	 to the topic, avoids	 offending anyone, and highlights	 three professional	 terms. Now	 you try	 it. Pick	 a familiar	 subject and write a series	 of jokes that are topic	 related and student oriented. Teachers	 Do It	 in Groups:	 Active	 Learning Humor	in	the	classroom 	can	be	more	than	a	joke	here	and	an	anecdote	there.	It can	be	an	innovative	approach.	Group	exercises,	for	example,	invite	lively interaction	by	their	very	nature. Show-and-tell 	is	a	device	created	by	grammar	schools	to	communicate	family secrets	to	thirty-two	other	families	before	9:15	in	the	morning. —Robert Orben When	humor	is	added	into	the	mix,	the	exercises	become	even	more	engaging and	the	lessons	more	memorable.	For	example,	you	can	spice	up	reading	scenes from 	Shakespeare’s	 Romeo	and	Juliet 	by	having	the	class	perform 	it as	a	rap,	or you	can	transform 	the	Gettysburg	Address	into	a	Dr.	Seuss-type	story. So,	I	used	to	be	a	music	teacher.	I	used	to	teach	K-5	music	here	in	New	York City.	I	taught 	the	recorder.	Are	you	guys	familiar	with	Satan's	little	flute?	If there's	music	in	Hell,	I	assure	you,	it 	is	played	on	a	recorder. —Ted	Alexandro Here	are	more	group	activities	with	a	humorous	spin: developing	a	class	Web	page	with	students’ 	jokes,	tongue	twisters,	riddles, funny	anecdotes,	and	nonsense	poems	and	songs drawing	an	event 	or	theory	as	a	comic	strip,	comic	book,	or	group	mural holding	mock	trials	for	notorious	historical 	figures acting	out 	the	functions	of	different 	body	parts creating	a	POW 	bulletin	board	or	scrapbook	with	homonyms,	euphemisms, and	double	entendres Even	junk	food	can	lend	itself	to	the	educational process: 	The	T.W.I.N.K.I.E.S. Project (Tests	 With	 Inorganic	 Noxious	 Kakes	 In	 Extreme	 Situations)	teaches the	scientific	method	via	experiments	with	Twinkies.	The	experiments (twinkiesproject.org)	consist 	mostly	of	abusing	Twinkies	in	extreme	situations (dropping	them from 	a	skyscraper,	bombarding	them 	with	radiation). Written	 in	 Fun Creative	writing	exercises	afford	more	opportunities	to	introduce	humor	into	the classroom 	and	can	make	a	mundane	assignment 	fresh	and	fun.	For	example, students	who	are	asked	to	create	humorous—yet 	factually	accurate—news headlines	for	past 	scientific	discoveries	will not 	only	learn	to	appreciate	a	subject in	a	new	way,	but 	also	hone	their	critical 	thinking	(and	headline	writing)	skills. Additional 	creative	writing	exercises	with	a	humorous	flair	include: modifying	proverbs,	nursery	rhymes,	or	maxims	with	subject-related	names and	principles composing	a	dictionary	of	fictitious	definitions	for	topical 	terms keeping	a	diary	for	an	object 	(a	day	in	the	life	of	a	pencil) creating	funny	words	or	phrases	for	remembering	course	concepts adding	new	captions	for	textbook	illustrations	and	photos drafting	a	complaint 	letter	from 	a	story	character	(defective	candles grievance	by	Jack	B.	Nimble) writing	intentional 	malaprops	for	famous	quotes transforming	conceptual 	principles	into	a	 Sesame	Street spot creating	a	dream 	diary	of	a	cartoon	character generating	crazy	predictions	based	on	existing	theories	or	principles writing	funny	last 	words,	nicknames,	bumper	stickers,	 Wanted 	posters, fortune	cookies,	or	resumes	for	historical 	figures. Blending	humor	into	a	writing	assignment 	has	a	hidden	benefit 	for	the	teacher— it 	provides	funny	material 	for	future	courses. Show and	 Tell Visual 	humor,	such	as	cartoons,	illustrations,	and	photographs,	works	especially well 	in	the	classroom; 	it 	seems	mandatory	that 	every	teacher	display	at 	least one Far	Side 	cartoon.	Captions	that 	accompany	the	visual 	(or	visuals	that 	accompany a	caption)	can	be	reformatted	as	punch	lines.	And	next 	to	recess,	students universally	love	movie	day. You	know	how	to	tell 	if	the	teacher	is	hungover?	Movie	Day. —Jay	Mohr Props,	such	as	puppets,	stuffed	animals,	or	costumes	can	enliven	a	lecture. Teachers	with	a	theatrical 	flair	can	dress	and	play	the	part 	of	a	historical 	figure— Abe	Lincoln	discussing	the	Civil 	War,	for	instance,	or	Albert 	Einstein	explaining physics.	As	Gail 	Goodwin	observed,	“Good	teaching	is	one-fourth	preparation and	three-fourths	theater.” Classroom	 of	 Fun An	inviting	classroom 	is	more	likely	to	increase	student 	participation	and promote	active	learning.	Creating	a	positive	and	fun	atmosphere	is	relatively easy,	especially	in	a	K-8	classroom.	Classroom 	humor	energizers	include: creating	a	“fun”	wall making	a	fake	yearbook using	funny	stickers,	stamps,	and	decals designing	fun	awards	and	certificates collecting	misinformation performing	random 	“fun”	acts assigning	brainstorming	fun	buddies building	an	age-appropriate	joke	collection compiling	funny	facts writing	funny	lunch	menus inventing	new	animals constructing	wanted	posters	of	fictional 	characters generating	crazy	predictions having	a	“backwards”	day The	 Writing	 Lab:	 What	 If	 Essays The two most important words	 in humor	 writing, what if, can breathe life into the dullest of all	 homework	 assignments: the essay. The what if? prompt can be used to inspire original	 and humorous creative writing assignments	 that encourage students	 to look	 at their subjects	 in a new	 light. For	 example, what if: fairy	 tale creatures	 appeared before Judge Judy	 (Pinocchio, for slander)? kids	 had written the Bill	 of Rights	 or	 the school	 rules? children’s	 stories	 were forced to be politically	 correct (Hansel, Gretel, and the codependent nonbiological	 mother)? Writing humor	 for	 the classroom	 is	 more than constructing one-liners —it also entails	 using humor	 to stimulate creativity. Take each of the following items	 and devise what if assignments. Compare your essay	 questions	 to the ones	 at the end of the chapter. animals movie characters maps periodic	 table cartoon characters Teachers	 Do It	 in the	 Cloud:	 Online	 Instruction Online	instruction	is	the	fastest 	growing	educational 	arena.	Schools,	at 	all 	levels, are	moving	from 	brick-and-mortar	classrooms	to	virtual 	learning.	Businesses also	recognize	the	advantages	of	e-learning—Writer’s	Digest,	the	publisher	of this	book,	offers	many	writing	courses	via	the	online	Writer’s	Digest 	University (www.writersonlineworkshops.com).	The	explosion	of	e-learning	creates additional 	markets	for	humor	writers.	As	Donna	Abernathy	observed,	“Online learning	is	not 	the	next 	big	thing,	it 	is	the	now	big	thing.” Educational 	experts	have	long	advocated	the	use	of	humor	in	the	traditional classroom.	However,	in	the	highly	structured	environment 	of	e-learning,	teachers and	trainers	have	fewer	tools	at 	their	disposal 	to	lighten	the	classroom atmosphere	or	promote	social 	interaction. People	expect 	to	be	bored	by	e-learning—let’s	show	them 	it 	doesn’t 	have	to	be like	that! —Cammy	Bean Although	online	learning	is	convenient,	many	students	find	it 	to	be	tedious	and boring.	Frank	LoSchiavo	and	I	published	the	first 	empirical 	study	of	the	planned, systematic	use	of	humor	in	online	instruction.	Our	findings	showed	that 	humor enhances	e-learning,	especially	the	social 	aspects	of	online	courses. Aim	 Cautiously I	failed	math	so	many	times	I	can’t 	even	count. —Francis	Stewart The	careful 	selection	of	humor	targets	for	e-learning	is	critical.	When	potentially “risky”	humor	is	used	in	the	traditional 	classroom,	the	teacher’s	delivery	can signal 	an	impending	joke	and	potentially	minimize	any	negative	reactions. Students	provide	immediate	feedback	that 	indicates	whether	the	humor	was suitable	and	should	be	used	again.	But 	in	the	virtual 	classroom,	humor	cannot be communicated	or	embellished	by	nonverbal 	cues,	and	e-learning	humor	can’t be easily	retracted.	It 	leaves	a	paper	trail 	for	the	PC 	police.	Therefore	instructors must 	carefully	consider	how	students	might react 	before	adding	humor	to	online instruction.	The	safest 	targets	for	the	online	classroom 	are	the	same	as	the traditional 	classroom—the	teacher	and	course	material. See	 the	 Funny Visual 	humor	is	especially	effective	in	e-learning.	Cartoons,	illustrations,	and photographs	can	easily	be	integrated	throughout 	an	online	course,	and	visuals can	be	used	as	a	punch	line	for	a	joke.	When	discussing	the	difficulty	of examinations,	a	setup	might 	be	“And	this	is	how	students	often	feel 	after	a	test …”	with	a	photograph	or	cartoon	of	frightened	individuals	serving	as	the	punch line.	If	the	visual 	is	hidden	behind	a	hyperlink,	the	funny	will 	be	maximized. Well,	tests	ain't 	fair.	Those	who	study	have	an	unfair	advantage. —Allan	Dare	Pearce Photographs	and	illustrations	can	be	doctored	with	editing	software	to	produce	a humorous	result,	and	it’s	easy	to	produce	whimsical 	GIFs	and	Vine	videos. Integrating	personal 	photographs	or	“home	movies”	into	online	instruction	can bring	a	more	personalized	and	intimate	feel 	to	the	often	sterile	e-learning experience. Embed	 the	 Funny The	placement 	and	duration	of	humor	used	in	online	lecture	modules	are	critical to	the	flow	of	instruction.	Short,	simple	jokes	are	most 	suitable	for	the introduction	and	can	include	a	funny	section	header,	a	photograph	or	illustration with	a	whimsical 	caption,	a	humorous	quote,	or	exaggerated	learning	goals. Longer	and	more	elaborate	humor	is	best 	suited	as	unit 	closers,	such	as	links	to funny	websites	or	YouTube	clips. Humor	permits	students	a	momentary	escape	from 	an	online	lecture,	and transitions	can	include	topic-related	tangents	or	self-deprecating	stories. Integrating	humorous	personal 	stories	or	“rants”	into	lecture	modules	also	offers the	chance	to	expand	on	previously	discussed	lecture	topics.	By	clearly identifying	the	tangent,	students	recognize	that 	the	rant 	is	separate	from 	the lecture.	And	because	the	commentaries	tangentially	support 	course	topics,	the rants	also	serve	as	teaching	opportunities. Fun	 for	 All Collaborative	projects	encourage	students	to	identify	and	screen	potential course material.	For	extra	credit,	students	could	find	sources	of	humor	on	any	course topic.	The	creation	of	discussion	boards,	like	an	“After	Hours	Club,”	would encourage	students	to	post 	jokes,	funny	observations,	or	course-relevant 	Web links. Tell 	me,	and	I'll 	forget.	Show	me,	and	I	may	remember.	Involve	me,	and	I'll understand. —Chinese	Proverb If	students	assist 	with	finding	material,	then	specific	guidelines,	such	as	the	three criteria	described	at 	the	beginning	of	the	chapter,	will 	be	needed.	Without 	clear.cut 	boundaries,	students	will 	likely	submit 	inappropriate	humor	including	an overabundance	of	tacky	selfies. Help	 Desk Experienced	e-learning	instructors	often	prepare	“canned”	responses	for	student problems	sent 	via	e-mail 	or	posted	on	a	discussion	board.	The	most 	common questions	concern	technical 	problems	and	might 	be	answered	with	lighthearted advice. Either	visit Adobe.com 	for	a	free	version	of	Acrobat 	Reader	or	buy	a	real computer	like	an	iMac. Take	a	deep	breath,	consume	an	adult 	beverage,	and	try	again.	If	you	still have problems,	then	drink	a	stronger	adult 	beverage	and	contact 	the	IT 	Help	Desk. As	Yoda	said,	“Do.	Or,	do	not.	There	is	no	try.” Humor	 in	 Action Although	online	learning	is	convenient,	many	students	find	it 	to	be	tedious	and boring.	To	address	this,	Frank	LoSchiavo	and	I,	with	assistance	from 	Matt Colemen,	published	the	first 	study	of	the	planned,	systematic	use	of	humor	in online	instruction.	Our	findings	showed	that 	humor	enhances	e-learning, especially	the	social 	aspects	of	online	courses.	The	following	examples	were used	in	an	online	context 	and	are	psychology	based,	yet 	they	illustrate	the	wide.ranging	potential 	of	humor	as	an	instructional 	strategy. Lecture	modules	contained	a	sprinkling	of	fictitious	items	based	on wordplay. Silly	Names Anita	Prozac I.M.	Sane Sy	Kosis Definitions Independent 	variable: 	doesn’t 	need	other	variables	to	feel good	about 	itself Double-blind	experiment: 	both	the	researcher	and	subjects	are	blindfolded Medications Anti-flunkotic: 	a	major	relaxant 	for	forgetting	a	really	bad	test score Cramonol: 	a	stimulate	for	pulling	all-nighters Examadril: 	a	memory	enhancer	for	studying Books Stalking,	Locating	Spies,	and	Other	Practical Uses	of 	Paranoid	Disorders Overcrowding:	The	Internal 	Life	of 	a	Multiple	Personality Unit 	closers	included	extended	humor	pieces	using	exaggeration. Questions	Yet	to	Be	Answered 	by	Psychologists Does	Play-Doh	promote	OCD? How	does	the	metric	system 	affect 	the	self-esteem 	of	inchworms? The	Real 	Warning	Signs	of	Obesity The	only	number	on	your	speed	dial 	is	Pizza	Hut Seven	times	daily	you	say,	“Super-size,	please!” You	receive	frequent 	flyer	miles	from 	Burger	King Triples	included	the	following. You	probably	will not 	pass	this	class	if	you	think	a	hard	drive	involves	an	SUV, if	you	go	to	the	bathroom 	to	matriculate,	or	if	you	wonder	why	all 	college principals	are	named	Dean. Seldom-used	yet 	practical 	applications	of	hypnosis	include	training	men	to	ask for	directions,	forgetting	your	last 	test 	score,	and	convincing	your	parents	that completing	a	college	degree	takes	seven	years. Pass, Fail, Or	 Laugh Exams	are	serious	business—just 	ask	third	graders	preparing	for	their	first Common	Core	test.	High	stakes	testing	is	happening	at 	every	level 	of	education and	is	adding	significant 	stress	to	students’ 	lives.	Fortunately	humor	can	lighten the	pressure	of	examinations	and	improve	performance. In	high	school,	I	could	not 	pass	a	math	test.	I	couldn’t 	pass	a	drug	test,	either. There	may	be	a	correlation. —Lynda	Montgomery The	occasional 	use	of	humor	in	examinations	is	appropriate	under	certain conditions.	Students	should	be	forewarned,	and	the	use	of	humor	must not interfere	with	their	ability	to	complete	the	test 	in	the	allotted	time.	Humor	can	be incorporated	into	examinations	in	several ways. I	never	did	very	well 	in	math—I	could	never	seem 	to	persuade	the	teacher	that I hadn't 	meant 	my	answers	literally. —Calvin	Trillin Test	 Directions Test 	directions	can	include	a	whimsical 	remark	or	silly	instruction. Use	of	a	No.	2	pencil 	because	No.	3	or	No.	4	pencils	may	cause	carpal 	tunnel syndrome. Keep	your	eyes	on	your	own	paper—it 	makes	your	writing	more	legible	for classmates. If	you	don’t 	have	a	No.	2	pencil,	you	may	use	two	No.	1	pencils. Lighthearted	quotes	could	also	be	added	to	test 	directions. I	can’t 	believe	it!	Reading	and	writing	actually	paid	off! —Homer	Simpson Hey	Google,	why	don't 	you	sit next 	to	me	during	my	exam? —Khat 	Leen A 	burp	is	not 	an	answer. —Bart 	Simpson Exam	 Questions Humor	can	be	injected	into	test 	questions	without 	causing	significant 	distraction. Application-based	questions	often	include	names	that 	can	be	reformatted	with cartoon,	fairy	tale,	movie,	or	television	characters. After	Charlie	missed	a	kick	during	an	important 	football 	game,	he	became depressed	and	vowed	to	quit 	the	team.	The	school 	psychologist 	challenged	his illogical 	reasoning	and	pointed	out 	that 	Mr.	Brown’s	“incompetence”	didn’t always	occur.	The	psychologist’s	response	was	most 	typical of	a	___________ therapist. a.	behavioral b.	psychoanalytic c.	client-centered d.	cognitive Self-deprecating	humor	also	works	in	exams	by	inserting	the	teacher’s	name	into test 	questions. Mark	is	continually	tense,	jittery,	and	apprehensive	for	no	specific	reason.	Dr. Shatz’s	diagnosis	would	probably	be: a.	phobia. b.	post-traumatic	stress	disorder. c.	obsessive-compulsive	disorder. d.	generalized	anxiety	disorder. Choice	 “e” Inserting	humor	into	test 	item 	choices	can	sometimes	be	distracting.	Yet ridiculous	answers	can	be	added	to	multiple-choice	questions	by	using	an additional 	distractor	(choice	“e”).	If	students	are	forewarned	that 	choice	“e”	can never	be	the	correct 	answer,	then	the	humor	will not 	interfere	with	performance. Which	of	the	following	behavior	occurs	the	most 	frequently? a.	schizophrenia b.	phobias c.	depression d.	OCD e.	Kardashian	selfies The	 “Final”	 Item Students	can	be	alerted	that 	the	test 	will 	conclude	with	a	humorous	question	(that will not be	graded). The	exam 	is	over	and	you a.	hope	this	was	a	bad	dream b.	should	have	read	the	book c.	probably	should	have	at 	least bought 	the	book d.	wonder	if	it’s	too	late	to	drop	the	course There	also	could	be	a	final 	course	item. This	is	the	last exam 	question	for	the	course.	Let’s	finish	the	class	as	a	winner. What 	comes	after	“c”? a.	Nope. b.	Still wrong. c.	Getting	closer. d.	Bingo: 	You’re	a	winner. The	 Writing	 Lab:	 Answers The following are possible essay	 questions. What if: animals	 could talk? movie characters	 sold items	 on e-Bay	 (for	 sale, ruby	 slippers, contact Dorothy)? maps	 were relabeled with funny	 names	 for	 geographic	 locations and landmarks? the periodical	 table was	 revised to include snack	 foods? cartoon characters	 applied for	 jobs	 (SpongeBob seeking fast-food employment)? Recap Comedians	entertain,	and	educators	educate,	so	humor	written	for	the	classroom must 	promote	instruction.	Otherwise	it 	may	be	considered	as	inappropriate	or fluff.	Humor	is	not 	a	pedagogical 	panacea,	and	the	mere	inclusion	of	humor	will not 	assure	that 	learning	will 	occur.	But 	when	used	properly,	humor	is	an invaluable	instructional 	tool 	for	reminding	teachers	and	students	that 	learning	is fun. Chapter	 14 That’s	a	Wrap Writing	is	easy.	All 	you	do	is	stare	at 	a	blank	sheet 	of	paper	until 	drops	of	blood form on	your	forehead. —Gene	Fowler Any	intelligent 	person	can	learn	humor,	work	at 	it,	and	even	produce	it.	The problem 	is	that 	the	commercial 	world	won’t pay	enough	for	second	best 	to	allow everyone	to	make	a	living.	In	humor,	good	enough	is	no	longer	good	enough. Only	a	few	of	those	who	study	humor	go	on	to	become	professional 	humorists, but 	the	same	is	true	in	many	professions. I	did	stand-up	comedy	for	eighteen	years.	Ten	of	those	years	were	spent 	learning, four	years	were	spent 	refining,	and	four	years	were	spent 	in	wild	success.	I	was seeking	comic	originality,	and	fame	fell 	on	me	as	a	by-product.	The	course	was more	plodding	than	heroic. —Steve	Martin Comedy	is	a	lot 	like	professional 	sports.	Past 	successes	are	history.	You	get 	paid for	today’s	hits.	One	difference	is	that 	in	baseball,	a	.300	hitter	earns	a	million dollars	and	the	fans	are	deliriously	happy	all 	season.	But 	a	.300	batting	average in	comedy	would	get 	professional 	performers	to	go	from 	boos	to	booze	in	a week. The	only	way	to	deal 	with	bombing	is	by	bombing	a	lot.	You	just 	learn	to	handle it.	I	guess	you	never	really	want 	to	bomb,	but 	if	it’s	not 	going	your	way,	then	you might 	as	well 	enjoy	the	ride	down. —Dave	Attell With	that 	kind	of	failure	rate,	you’d	think	any	person	who	had	reached	the	age	of reason	would	take	up	plumbing.	Yet,	writing	and	performing	humor	is	rising	in popularity.	There	are	simply	not 	enough	qualified	writers	today	to	fill 	the increasing	need.	Besides	the	standard	venues,	more	and	more	markets	are begging	for	humor	material: 	speeches,	blogs,	newsletters,	advertising,	social media,	and	online	education.	Moreover,	if	you’re	successful,	the	money	in comedy	is	so	plentiful 	that 	professional 	practitioners	are	like	well-endowed actors	in	a	porn	movie—“You	mean	I	get 	paid	for	doing	that?” Since	you’re	only	as	good	as	your	last 	joke,	there’s	considerable	insecurity in	comedy	writing—and	a	great deal 	of	turnover.	There	are	six	ways	that you	can improve	your	chances	of	succeeding	as	a	professional 	humor	writer. 1.	Work 	With 	Others. 	Write	with	a	partner	whenever	possible.	Despite	the added	difficulty	of	scheduling,	teams	of	two	or	three	writers	spark	each	other’s wit 	and	test 	and	refine	each	other’s	ideas.	“I	love	working	with	other	writers,” wrote	humorist 	Phillip	Lasker.	“I	have	learned	to	appreciate	surrounding	myself with	talent.	Others	may	have	better	lines	than	you	or	better	story	points.	You have	to	listen	to	those	you	respect,	and	it’s	also	fun	to	notice	that 	the	great writers	are	listening	to	you.” As	Eric	Idle	of	the	six-member	Monty	Python	group	observed: Getting	six	guys	to	agree	on	what’s	funny	is	easy.	We	read	it 	aloud.	If	we	laugh, it’s	in; 	if	we	don’t,	it’s	out.	If	four	guys	think	something’s	funny	and	two	guys think	it’s	not,	we	solve	that 	very	simply: 	We	take	the	two	guys	out 	and	kill 	’em. Often	a	student 	will 	point out 	that 	a	single	person,	like	Charlie	Chaplin	or Neil 	Simon,	wrote	the	most 	famous	comedies.	Well,	if	you’re	as	good	as	Chaplin or	Simon,	you	can	do	it 	alone,	too! 2.	Hire	An 	Agent. 	Agents	are	a	great 	example	of	a	catch-22	situation.	The	big agents	won’t 	touch	unknowns,	but 	beginners	can’t 	become	known	without an agent.	There	are	many	exceptions,	however,	and	your	job	is	to	find	them. Playwright 	Abe	Burrows	said	he	thought 	of	his	agent 	as	family.	When	Burrows paid	his	agent 	10	percent,	he	didn’t 	think	of	it 	as	a	commission,	but 	as	sending money	home	to	mother. Little	by	little	the	agents	have	taken	over	the	world.	They	don't 	do	anything,	they don't 	make	anything,	and	they	just 	stand	there	and	take	their	cut. —Jean	Giraud Stephen	King	believes	that successful 	writing	calls	for	talent,	a	self-critical eye, and	the	willingness	to	mercy	kill 	bad	material.	He	also	recommends	that 	writers follow	his	First 	Rule	of	Writers	and	Agents: 	“You	don't 	need	[an	agent]	until you're	making	enough	for	someone	to	steal,	and	if	you're	making	that 	much, you'll 	be	able	to	take	your	pick	of	good	agents.” When	you	reach	the	point 	of	needing	professional 	help,	names	and addresses	of	agents	can	be	obtained	by	searching	the	Internet.	Just 	never	give	a new	agent 	your	wallet 	to	hold	when	you	go	onstage. 3.	Test,	Test,	Test. 	Humor	can’t 	be	tested	in	a	vacuum.	You	need	an	audience, and	it 	must 	be	an	audience	that’s	receptive	to	humor.	If	you	can’t 	find	an audience,	try	your	jokes	out 	on	another	humor	professional—writer	or performer.	Don’t 	walk	up	to	a	stranger	and	ask,	“What 	d’ya	think	of	this?”	The only	thing	worse	than	that 	is	trying	your	humor	out 	on	your	friends,	spouse, parents,	or	children.	They	are	too	subjective,	too	critical,	and—instead	of	just relaxing	and	enjoying	it—they	turn	into	pseudo-analysts. There	are	a	hell 	of	a	lot 	of	jobs	that 	are	scarier	than	live	comedy.	Like	standing in	the	operating	room 	when	a	guy’s	heart 	stops,	and	you’re	the	one	who	has	to fix	it. —Jon	Stewart Only	one	out 	of	every	ten	jokes	will 	probably	work	the	first 	time	out.	Moreover, no	joke	will 	ever	please	every	person	in	the	audience.	It’s	impossible.	Getting laughs	from 50	percent 	of	the	audience	is	doing	very	well. If	you’re	writing	material 	for	public	speaking,	put 	thirty	seconds	of	new material at 	the	very	beginning	of	a	tested	speech.	The	first 	thirty	seconds	are	the toughest 	because	that’s	when	the	audience	is	most 	skeptical,	so	if	the	new material 	goes	over	then,	you	know	it 	has	merit. If	I	get 	big	laughs,	I’m 	a	comedian.	If	I	get 	little	laughs,	I’m 	a	humorist.	If	I	get no	laughs,	I’m 	a	singer. —George	Burns Jokes	are	like	machine-gun	bullets.	They	don’t 	all 	hit 	the	target,	but 	if	you	shoot enough	of	them 	accurately	at 	the	audience,	you’ll 	kill 	’em.	Count 	one	point for	a twitter	of	laughter,	two	points	for	a	solid	laugh,	and	three	points	for	applause.	If a	joke	doesn’t 	score	any	points	after	at 	least 	three	attempts,	throw	it 	out.	If	it 	gets only	one	point,	try	rewriting	it 	so	your	score	is	constantly	going	up. 4.	Write,	Write,	Write. 	Writing	humor	is	an	all-day	assignment,	because	new ideas	can	pop	into	your	head	anytime,	anywhere.	Some	writers	feel 	humor	can be	conceived	even	when	they	dream,	so	be	sure	to	keep	a	notebook	by	your	bed. Once	you’ve	learned	the	basic	techniques,	don’t 	let 	anybody	talk	you	out of writing	your	own	way.	Humor	styles	change	with	each	generation,	and	while formulas	rarely	vary,	standard	subject 	matter,	formats,	fads,	and characterizations	are	constantly	being	challenged.	New	ideas	are	the	lifeblood	of comedy,	as	they	are	of	most 	businesses.	And	most 	new	ideas	take	at 	least several years	to	germinate. 5.	See,	Hear,	Speak 	Funny. 	In	addition	to	luck	and	perseverance,	your	success as	a	humor	writer	depends	on	four	things: Watching. 	Look	for	the	absurdities	of	life.	Notice	the	physical 	actions	that bring	a	smile	to	people’s	lips. Reading. 	If	you	read	something	funny,	make	a	note	of	it.	Notice	the construction.	Keep	adding	to	your	joke	file. Listening. 	Try	to	remember	how	people	phrase	things; what Mel 	Brooks calls	“the	rhythm 	of	human	speech.”	Things	that 	look	good	on	paper	don’t always	perform 	well.	We	don’t 	speak	in	full 	sentences,	we	often	skip	words, and	we	usually	use	contractions. Speaking. 	Do	your	own	stand-up.	Don’t 	hesitate	to	deliver	your	own material 	in	a	meeting,	at 	private	parties,	or	to	dinner	guests.	You’ll 	notice how	audiences	differ,	how	your	performance	differs,	and	how	important 	it is	to	have	the	right 	material 	for	the	right 	audience. 6.	Have	Fun. 	Urban	legend	claims	that 	actor	Edmund	Kean	uttered	the following	on	his	deathbed: 	“Dying	is	easy.	Comedy	is	hard.” It 	doesn’t 	matter	whether	the	tale	is	fact 	or	fiction—the	quote	is	accurate. Comedy	is	hard,	and	it 	takes	years	of	practice,	dedication,	and	rejection	to	be	a successful 	humor	writer. Yet,	life	is	brief,	and	you	might 	as	well 	enjoy	the	journey.	Therefore	our final 	advice	is	simple.	Think	funny,	write	funny,	and	have	fun. In	the	end,	everything	is	a	gag. —Charlie	Chaplin
